Uh let's print them out in hex with a space after them. So that's just going to print out all the code points spaced out in hex. And then we can just do my wide str indexed to i. And then let's drop in a new line at the end just to make things look nicer. And so now if we compile it, we come in here and we run it. You can see sure enough we get all the code points. You know the regular asky ones which are only they're shorter. They're only a bite long. I mean they're stored as four bytes. It's only it's not printing out the leading zeros. But then my emoji at the end, you can see it has its code point here. And so this works. And so anytime you're processing on a per character or per symbol basis, white characters are really easy, right? Because it's just an array of code points. So you can go through and you can index really easily. If you want to find the fifth code point, you just jump to index 5. You don't have to do any kind of searching through the string. You can just go to the index that you care about. If we were on the other hand using a variable length encoding scheme like UTF8, then this would be a bit more complicated and more about that in a future video. I'm definitely going to get to UTF8 cuz I think it's pretty cool because it's super popular and it's really cool. Also, the main downside of wide character arrays is that these wide character strings are big. So, the string up here is mostly made up of ASKI characters like we printed out down here, right? These things that can be stored possibly in one bite. But we are now using because we're using wch_t, we are using four bytes per character. And so we're using about four times as much memory per symbol as we would otherwise have to use. And so you don't find a lot of people storing their text in wide character format for long periods of time or sending wide strings across the network because it just requires a lot more space and bandwidth. Of course, the exception to this might be if you speak only an emoji or if you are using a language that has code points with much higher value, then you might actually not lose much. And so this might be a reasonable thing to do. If you are primarily working with English text, this is probably a very inefficient way to store text. I don't want to have to have separate rules for every single module in my project. as projects get big, that can get really tedious. And then right here, I'm basically just using those and doing a substitution. I'm substituting C for O to get a list of the O files that I'm eventually going to build. And then finally down here, this is the name of my final binary. It's going to be greenhouse.f. And so all this stuff is stuff that I've shown you in a previous video. But this setup has a problem, and that is that it doesn't handle headers properly. So if I come in here and I compile it, yeah, it's going to go through and compile everything the first time. If I try to compile it again, it's going to say, okay, nothing's to be done cuz it's already built everything. But let's say that I come in here and I make some change to this header file. So, I modify this file in some way and I come in here and compile it. It still thinks there wasn't any modification. Well, it's not that it doesn't think there was any modification. It just doesn't know that any of these modules depend on this header file. And so, what I want to do today is show you how you can make your make file handle these header dependencies properly. Because really, when you change a header file, I would really like to recompile everything that depends on that header. And we're going to do that today with just one little compiler option. And then as we're going to come down here when we are compiling our C files to our files, I'm going to add -mmd all uppercase. And yes, this is only going to work only guaranteed to work on clang and GCC, but most of you are using that anyway. So this is going to work for just about everybody. So this is what I'm going to do. Now, if I come in here and I make clean and recompile, now you're going to notice something new, and that is that for each of these modules, we have a D file that was created. Okay, this is a dependency file. And if we open one of these up, you can see that these contain a make rule. In this case, a make rule for creating this modules.0 file and all of its dependencies. So it has the C file in here and it has all the header files that this C file includes. Okay, so that's really handy. If only we could just get this rule into our make file, then we would be good to go. And we can. It's super easy. So let's just jump back in here. Now, what I'm going to do is come down here and make a depths variable. That's for dependencies. And we're going to do just like we did before, but we're going to take C2 to D. Okay. So, this is basically going to give us a list of D files that correspond to our C files. And then if I just come down here, I can just say include depths. So what this is going to do is it's going to include all of these D files into my make file and they have all the dependency information and also before I forget let's come down here and also make sure that we add to our clean target uh removing our dependencies. Okay. So now if I come in, okay, so we say make, it's nothing's can be done. But if I come back in here to my header and let's say I add some white space, I change something about it and I compile. Now, it detects that change and it says, "Oh, there are actually some modules that depend on this internal temp.h and it recompiles them properly." And this is really helpful, especially when you have a large project with a lot of different modules that are each depending on each other in different ways. And so I hope that's helpful. These two modules depend on each other. And so now if we come in here and we compile it and we run it. Well, okay. So first of all, you're going to see that yes, module 2 is using module one or module one is using module two. Sorry, numbers are hard. Uh, and this is happening before we're starting at before things get started. And this is actually just fine because of the order, right? Module 2 is initialized before module one. So, we're okay. But what happens if we revert this back to where it was and now the order is switched. So, if I come in here now, you notice I'm trying in module one to use module 2 and it's not initialized. So, we're getting an error message, right? So, we could have problems. And you're going to see module dependencies like this with uh anytime you have like a low-level module that's handling something like memory allocation or something that is a building block that other modules can actually use, you can run into this problem where you need to initialize those lower level modules first before you start doing things with the other modules that use them. And so anytime you have module dependencies, you want to be very careful. It's very fragile to say, "Hey, my code works." You just have to guarantee that you add that you link them in a certain order. or the constructors show up in a certain order that is really going to be annoying. So what can we do about this? Well, one thing you'll notice if you look at the documentation for the constructor attributes is that they can also take an optional priority argument. So you can specify lower priority or higher priority values and that can change the order in which they run in theory. Now in practice this doesn't really help us very much and the reason is well first it works only within a single translation unit. So I compiled these modules. When I compile them, they are compiling in three separate translation units. And so the priority in one of these translation units is not going to matter in terms of the priorities in other translation units, right? That's not going to give you any kind of control there necessarily. I mean, it might some compilers might do that, but you're not guaranteed to get ordering. The second issue is even if priority values did work across different modules, across different translation units, I would have to keep track and know the priority value of every module out there that I might have to work with. And that also seems to be very fragile and errorprone, right? Especially if I'm bringing in a module, someone else's module, and they have their own priority scheme. I may have to go through and actually tweak their code and things to make it work with mine. That's going to be annoying. So if we are making a library or module that requires an initialization function be run and that module might be used by another modules constructor then what do we do? Well I mean this is probably the most portable and safest way to do this. It's not sexy but like let's say we know we're going to need you know let's start with module 2 cuz that's the one that module one depends on. Now this is going to work regardless of the order that our modules are being used and how they depend on each other because we're always before we use it we're going to check to see if initialization has happened. So just double check if we Oh but now we have a problem guys. What did I do wrong? Oops. Sorry. module two in it. Like I said, one and two, these two modules that differ by just a digit is confusing me. Um, so yeah, so now we're now we're okay. Now, one thing you will notice here is now module 2 is being initialized twice because it's being initialized when we first try to use it and then because of this constructor attribute. ## 26. The two main types of network socket
**Channel:** Jacob Sorber | **Views:** 11K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 6:32 | **ID:** HpAdCgs9HCI
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=HpAdCgs9HCI

### Transcript:
today I want to talk about the different kinds of sockets that you can run into when you're doing Network programming now if you've done any network programming at all you've probably run into sockets I probably and I've done some Network programming videos in the past I'll link to them down in the description if you missed them but they use sockets and I'm thinking about making some additional socket videos in the future and those are Works in progress but today I just wanted to take a second to make a quick video to talk about the different types of sockets you may run into because there are two main types you're going to see now before we get too deep in there just a quick overview what is a socket just in case those have missed it it's well it's is it a data structure not really it's more of an abstraction it's an interface that the operating system gives you that allows you to perform network communications in a somewhat standard way and it works a lot like a file basically I say hey I want a new socket and it's going to give me a socket descriptor which is going to be an integer that represents that socket so it's going to feel a lot like files only that number rather than representing a file that number is going to represent a network endpoint and that's an endpoint that I could use to either send data to someone either I could be on the same machine or it could be on the other side of the world or to receive data from some other process and if you haven't seen sockets before you really should look into them because like I said they are probably the closest thing we have to a standard network interface when we are working in C and of course you see sockets in other languages as well okay so that's what a socket is now for what we're getting at today which is the two different kinds of socket because you are going to run into two different kinds of sockets the first is the stream socket and the second is the datagram socket and I'm sure some of you back there are going well what about Unix domain sockets or raw sockets and yeah let me know if you want to see videos about those that's not what I'm talking about today today I'm just talking about the types of sockets and the interfaces that they provide now in my previous Network programming videos where we talked about sockets we were using stream sockets that's the first type and a stream socket is going to make communication it's going to make your communication connection look like a stream like a stream of data a stream of bytes you can either put data into the stream or you can pull data out of the stream and when I try to pull data out of the stream the btes are going to be in the same order that they went in now keep in mind that if I send a big block of data across the network that data is going to get broken up arbitrarily into packets the you know small chunks that are going to be sent across the network but if we're working with stream sockets we don't see that chunking up of the data we don't see any of that it just looks like data went in data comes out we get this nice continuous stream of data now you're also going to hear people refer to stream sockets as reliable sockets and that may not mean exactly what you think it means these sockets these connections that we set up between a client and a server can still be broken they can still get disconnected like if the Wi-Fi goes down or someone unplugs a network cable or things just get too congested and we can't get packets through what reliable means in this case is that let's say under the hood we break up this big chunk of data into individual packets and let's say that some of those packets get lost or maybe two of them get received in a different order you know when a was sent before B but B is actually received first and then a is received later well with reliable stream sockets basically a lost packet is going to be resent it's going to try to resend it and any packets that get out of order will be put back in the right order before they get delivered to your application and all of this stuff is being handled for you magically by the transport layer protocol usually TCP that is taking care of all of this messy stuff down under the hood with stream sockets you don't see any that and that's pretty nice now why would we ever want anything else well sometimes we don't want all that resending and congestion control and reordering of packets we don't want that for one reason or another maybe I'm writing a program where I don't care about the ordering or I don't care if a little piece of data gets lost because all of that reliability stuff that TCP does for you that comes at a cost and so if you don't need it or you don't want it some applications just choose to not use all these features for example let's say that I have a program that's is going to send audio and video data across maybe I'm I'm writing something like Zoom some kind of video chat application is sending audio and it's sending video now what happens if one of my audio or video packets gets lost on the way well if I'm using a stream socket TCP is going to try to resend that packet and depending on how fast or slow my network is that might actually add some lag because it's like it makes everything weight until I can get through this next piece of data that it Lost And if what I care about is a really great experience experience for the user on the other end this really could be the wrong move instead what I might want to do is just say hey you didn't get that little chunk of data that's fine I'm just going to keep sending the more recent data and yeah so one frame got a little messed up not a big deal that's 13th of a second or 124 of a second depending on the frame rate of your video one little garbled frame that's hardly noticeable where if we take the time to resend a whole packet and slow everything down you might find yourself with a very noticeable very annoying glitch in your audio and video and of course we can't do that with stream sockets but we can do that with datagram sockets now with datagram sockets we are sending individual packets or datagrams if you want to sound pretentious and we don't get any of that reliability stuff no reordering no retransmissions and no congestion control so I'm sending these packets or datagrams and those packets could get lost on the way maybe they get received out of order the network is still going to make a best effort attempt to get my data through but if packets get lost they get lost life's tough also recall that I mentioned that there is no congestion control and so what that also means is that there it's it's never going to it's not going to throttle back any of my packets if I'm sending too many or there's too many people talking on the network it's going to leave that entirely up to me and so I can send as much data as fast as I want now of course that can have problems if a bunch of us are just trying to send too much data we can clog up the network and cause all sorts of problems so when we are using our datagram sockets it's best to be on our best behavior and you know make sensible decision so we don't ruin the party for everyone and you know also for our application but if we play nice I can sometimes actually get more data through the network than I could if I'm using TCP and stream sockets and while datagram sockets aren't usually the ones you start with I think they're really cool and I have some upcoming videos that I am planning to do on datagram sockets and UDP that's the protocol that they use but they're not quite ready so today I just thought I'd do this quick overview help explain that there are two different types hopefully it's helpful to some of you hopefully you learned something new today and I'll see you soon

able to see what happens to make sure it still compiles and if we run it you can see okay so we've got we got these reads that are happening and they're printing out the text but you can see that when the rights are happening when we're actually updating the text we're getting these weird partial strings you know that where where it we get half of one and half of the other and that's because we're not protecting any of these shared data structures we're just letting things happen concurrently however they want to happen and so you see the updates because they take a long time and I want to note that even if we didn't slow it down if we had kept it at full speed you would still see these kinds of par updates but you would have had like millions of lines of output really quick and that's just a pain to work with and it's interesting sometimes when you have this this kind of race condition uh it's going to basically cause you to crash in this case we're not crashing we're just getting output that's not what we want we'd really like a a whole intact string or nothing so we have a thread safety issue and we need to fix it now the way we have looked at doing this before in other examples was to use a mutex lock so for example we can come up here and we can say let's make a pthread m mutex T and let's just call it lock and we'll set it equal to pthread mutex initializer yeah this is just the default initializer that pthreads provides for mutex locks and then all we have to do is come down to our readers and our writers and let's just say Okay down in here we're going to say pthread mutex lock and we're going to pass in our lock like that and then once we're done then we can come down here and we can unlock it okay so that is pretty straightforward we got to do it down here with our writer as well so right here before we start to slow copy we will grab the lock and then when we're done we will unlock the lock okay so now if I come down and I compile it and we run it we should be able to see that now we are getting basically complete strings you're not getting these partial strings anymore so that's nice but we do have a performance problem that uh you may not see right now so just to illustrate that here let me kill this ustr it let's go up here and let's actually slow down our read just a little bit so rather than just having this half a second between each of these let's actually slow down our reads a bit so let's make our reads maybe take uh quarter of a second and then we'll put H we'll leave it half a second between okay so now this is going to make read take a while now what's going to happen you remember that a mutex lock can only basically one thread can hold the mutex lock at a time so what's going to happen here You' got these four readers and when I run it you're going to notice that the readers are going to line up so zero gets to read one gets to read They're each going to you know it's slowed down a little bit right so so they're each basically taking turns and this is really wasteful because it's actually totally fine if all these readers read at the same time the only thing that's dangerous is if you read when an update is taking place or maybe even worse if you had two writers that were trying to write at the same time that could be bad too but even though it's perfectly safe for all the readers to access at the same time the mutex lock will not allow it mutex locks give you Mutual exclusion so one thread at a time can be accessing that buffer and so it's going to be slow and say I had a thousand readers that all wanted to read at the same time they would all have to queue up and only one at a time could read and that's going to be really that's really going to slow things down in a way that we're not going to be happy with in a lot of real applications where you have a lot of threads that are all accessing a shared resource and they're not modifying it so there really is no safety concern now this is where reader writer locks or read write locks come in and with a reader writer lock the difference is I grab the lock as either a reader or a writer and if a bunch of readers want to get the lock at the same time no problem they just they do their thing and we're fine but if a writer grabs the lock then readers have to wait no readers can basically come in no nobody else can grab the lock when a writer gets the lock they're the only thread that can get the lock so let's make this change really quick and we can see what happens here let me just go through and kill this so if we come up here the only things we're going to change here we'll just we'll leave this in place so you can see what it looked like before but basically what we're looking at here is you've got pthread reader writer lock T right here and then here we're going to change pthread read write lock initializer so looks very similar we've just change mutex to RW lock in both cases so now we have a new kind of lock but we do have to change one thing down here so we have to come well couple things we have to come down here and say okay read write lock and then what we're going to do here here is say we want to grab the lock as a reader so we want to get a read lock on this and then when we want to unlock we don't actually have to tell it because it it knows we're reader when we unlock it so we're grabbing it as a reader here that's great and then we unlock it when we're done so the Locking actually is exactly the same except we just had to specify am I a reader or am I a writer and then down here in my writer code we will do the same thing down here except we want to grab this as a writer and this goes to unlock okay so at this point our program should run basically exactly the same except that in the slow reads what you're going to see up here is that during this period of time when reading is just at unnecessarily slow you're going to be able to see all of these threads are going to much more quickly just be able to access the shared Resource as soon as it's available they're not going to have to wait for the other readers to finish okay so if we come in here we recompile and we run it so you can see basically 0 through three they're basically hitting it at exactly the same time as soon as the writer's done they're in there and they're reading and so they're able to get instant access now note in this it is really important that we make sure that we are following the rules right let's say that I came down here and you know I grab the lock as a reader but I don't read I I actually modify the data structure well bad things are going to happen right because I'm not following the rules so we are it's basically it's you know it's it's our duty as the programmer to make sure that if I'm going to grab a reader writer lock as a reader that I really am only reading the data structure I'm not modifying anything and of if I use a reader writer lock but I always just grab it as a writer well it's going to function essentially like a mutex lock because that's you know that's how mutex locks work you're going to have one thread at a time will be able to get access to the data structure and so it'll it'll behave exactly the same so at this point students often ask is there an advantage to using mutex locks at all why don't we just always use reader writer locks and just grab the lock as a writer if we need mutex lock type behavior and of course you totally could do that the primary reason I would recommend not doing that is that one when you bring in reader writer locks the people reading your code your co-workers whatever they see that and they think oh we have a reader writer scenario where there are different kinds of threads and they're accessing the data structure in different ways and that may confuse them the other thing is is that mutex locks are simpler they're the implementation if you look at the code they have less logic in them so they probably are just a hair faster than using a reader writer lock and grabbing it in right mode but aside from a little bit of coworker confusion and probably a tiny performance penalty you're probably going to see the same behavior if you use mutex locks as if you use reader writer locks but only grab the right lock so that's reader writer locks I hope that's helpful I hope you learn something new today and until next time I'll see you later

happens great but for now that Foundation is C and so understanding C and being good at C is actually really really valuable so that's reason number two now let's talk about the one that I actually ## 46. Is it concurrent or parallel?
**Channel:** Jacob Sorber | **Views:** 30K | **Date:** 2 years ago | **Duration:** 3:48 | **ID:** r2__Rw8vu1M
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=r2__Rw8vu1M

### Transcript:
today I want to talk about the difference between concurrent and parallel [Music] welcome back everybody thanks for being here and thanks for all you do to support this Channel today I want to look at two concepts the idea of concurrency and parallelism these are similar ideas or sort of similar but there are also ideas that new students student programmers often confuse in fact sometimes even veterans in fact if I make enough videos eventually I will use these incorrectly and one of you are going to call me out on it I can just guarantee it but today I wanted to talk about them just to try to make your lives a little easier and help you sort them out to try to bring a little more Precision into our discourse when we're talking about software and how our programs work also different people's definitions on these terms made very slightly I'm going with mine and what I've seen in the field and what seems to be typical but if your operating systems Professor happens to have a different set of definitions please go with theirs and at least until the class is over and then of course you can tell them they're wrong so what is the difference well let's start with the idea of parallelism so parallelism simply put means that there are two things happening at the same time okay so sometimes we think about parallel computing rotation that would mean that two computations are occurring at the same instant in time now typically this requires some kind of Hardware support right so this could be two tasks that are being executed simultaneously on two different cores right they're happening at the same time so literally you could have two things happening at the same instant in time and if I take two tasks let's say each task takes a second and I run them in parallel I should get them done more quickly because they're actually happening at the same time as long as they don't depend on one another but if they depend on one another then they're not going to be really done in parallel because one of them is going to have to wait while the other one finishes okay so parallelism means computation is happening at the same time and this isn't just about processor tasks this could also be two different operations like reading from a disk the disk may be servicing a read request while I'm doing computational tasks and those two things could be happening at the same time because they depend on two different pieces of Hardware that are each able to work independently of each other also of course you can have computation being done on separate machine separate servers you see this a lot in big super computers compute clusters that have many processors so yeah multiple cores multiple processors it's all parallel now concurrent is a little different if two tasks are concurrent that means that they start and do their work and end in overlapping periods of time okay so let's say that I have a processor that has a single core just one core it can only really do one computational task at a time I can still run many processes on that task and moderate operating systems are simply going to set timers and every time the timer fires they quickly switch tasks so they'll do a little bit of your word processor and then a little bit of work on your web browser and a little bit of work on your video game and it appears that these things are all happening at the same time but really what's happening is the processor is jumping the core is jumping from one task to another really quickly faster than you can see and so many people might be tempted incorrectly to say this is happening in parallel it's not happening in parallel it's actually sequential but just chopping it up into little blocks but because these things have the appearance of happening at the same time because their their starts and ends overlap we refer to these as concurrent now of course be careful here some people like to think of these as two totally separate classes but they're not concurrency includes parallelism so if two tasks are parallel they're also concurrent right because their start and end times overlap it's just that not all concurrent tasks are actually done in parallel so anyway I hope that helps I hope you learned something new today clarified some things helped some of you on your operating systems exams hopefully this helps you be more precise in future conversations and until next week I'll see you later

what this is doing is actually compiling the checks right into my program and so now you can see it comes in here and it says hey there was this read of size one so one byte at this particular address which you know may not look like much to you but once you get used to things but once you get used to programming a long time you might look at this and say oh that looks like a local variable but even if you don't notice it it is going to give you the stack frame right here no it is going to tell you information like this is on the stack which thread it's running on and the offset from a particular frame okay so this is all being produced by a tool called address sanitizer which is basically putting a bunch of code into my code which is checking all of my memory accesses and then also down here it actually gives me a what's called a it's a it's a summary of The Shadow memory now I don't have time in this video to dig into Shadow memory let me know if you'd like to see a video on key that that gives information about how this memory is being used you know what is this region that's being messed up and that can often give you insight into what my program did that was wrong so that's pretty cool basically we didn't have to do anything except change how we compile our program we just had to add this sanitize equals address option to the compiler and everything just happened right now so that's really cool but keep in mind that nothing or almost nothing in this world is free so this does come at a cost in this case it's a performance cost because the compiler is inserting a bunch of memory checks throughout my code and so if we come down here and we look let's say we take a look at the size of my binary you can see okay we've got a pretty good size binary here if I come down here and I remove this and we make clean recompile you can see so like I have a much smaller binary at this point than if we come back here add it back in so we may claim it may compile you're seeing okay so it's still bigger but with GCC it's not nearly as big as it was with clang this is just coming from the fact that these compilers are inserting these checks in different ways and clang's probably including a bunch of code statically maybe I don't know I'd have to look deeper into it but the point is this will vary a bit by compiler but either way the code is a bit bigger and if we timed it you would see that the code is also a little bit slower because it's having to make these checks you know nothing's free so what you often see in commercial code is that address sanitizer will be turned on during debugging now your debug builds will have this turned on because it gives you lots of really rich information about what your program's doing and what it's doing incorrectly while you are trying to figure things out and then if you need that binary to be lean and mean and really small when you ship it out then you can take this out in your release build if you don't know what I'm talking about with debug and release builds well check out some of my my build system I make more complicated make videos I do talk about how to produce two different binaries for afraid of those nasty memory corruption bugs that can otherwise be a real pain to find with tools like address sanitizer they're really not that bad so I hope this helps hope you learned something new
